Nothing is removed by itself, as an Elektron never unloads a slot unasked. **The format:** `FORMAT_VERSION` is 6, `Track.sample` is gone, and there's no migration, so songs saved before, such as the test projects in `~/Music/vimtracker/projects/`, need saving again from a matching version or making again. **Left out:** slices and marks (#270, #271), per-file defaults (#278), and finding a moved file anywhere but the project's `samples/`. Both golden renders are unchanged, as a gain of 0 dB and a tune of 0 multiply by exactly 1. Added on 2026-11-09.
95. **A track's `Reset` starts its chain again every so many of the song's steps, counted from where its chain last started, and `Space a` does it once by hand for the cursor's track, on the launch grid** (#44, M19's sequencer logic). **Prior art:** Elektron's master length, which sends every track back to its start after up to 1024 steps while each track loops at its own length, here per track, as the Polyend forum post the issue quotes asks, so a 5-step part reset every 16 makes one fixed, syncopated bar while a 7-step part drifts against it. **The issue's worry** that the engine keeps one place for all tracks is out of date: since `Speed` (decision 82) each track has its own clock, count and chain start, and the phrase view's other-track columns, its playheads and recording all work from those, so a track's reset needs no new place, only its chain started again. **The setting:** `Reset` on the Sound page's right-hand column under `Speed`, as both are about the track's time, `Track.reset` as a number of steps from 1 to 1024, the most Elektron's master length has, `reset` in files and left out when off. It shows `Off`, whole bars as `:launch` names them, such as `1bar`, and anything else in steps, such as `5-step`; the status line says `its chain starts again every 5 steps of the song`. `i` takes steps, in hex after `0x`, bars such as `2bar`, or `off`; `=` and `-` move it a step, down from 1 to off; `+` and `_` move it to the next whole bar, below a bar to off; and `x` turns it off. It isn't a step's lock or in `K`'s list: it's a schedule for the track rather than a value that changes from step to step, as a section's `:reset` isn't. **What it counts:** the song's sixteenths, whatever the track's speed, so `1bar` is a bar at `1/8` too, as `:reset` and launches count sixteenths (decision 82). It counts from where the track's chain last started: the top, a section bringing in a new chain, a launch, a realign, or its own last reset, so it's always that many steps of the chain, and a launched part keeps its bar from its launch. That's where the track's clock last lined up, which the engine already keeps, so the check is a subtraction. A track playing from a row of its chain counts it from that chain's top. On a reset that falls partway through a slower track's step, the step is cut short, as at a section's start. **A chain start:** a reset starts the chain as a section bringing it in does: from its top with its lanes in line, conditions counting passes from the first (decision 84), accumulators set to `Chain` or to loops starting again (decision 85), and the phrase's walk starting again (decision 91). **The groove carries on**, unlike `:reset` and `Space A`, which start every track's count again together: a track's groove counts its steps from the song's start or the last of those (decision 80), so a track's reset doesn't move which of the song's sixteenths it swings, however odd the reset. With the bar a reset usually is, the two are the same. **With a section's `:reset`:** both apply. The section's realigns every track, and a track's reset counts from where its chain last started, so it counts again from the section's: with `Reset` at 8 and `:reset 12`, the track starts again at 8, 12, 20, 24 and so on. Where they fall together, the section's realigns everything and the track starts once, so neither wins over the other. A section that starts with `reset` is the same. **Playing from a step** hears what playing up to it would: working out the start walks each track's resets as it walks the sections. Auditioning a row while stopped takes the track's reset into account. **`Space a`:** the cursor's track's chain starts again at the next line of the launch grid, once, as `Space A` does for every track (decision 92), and a second press takes it back. It's the lowercase of the pair, as `Space m` is to `Space M`. Like a reset, it's a chain start that keeps the groove counting, so the track comes back in line with the song's swing; `Space A` landing on the same line takes it with it. It works only while playing, says `Realign works while playing` when stopped and `Return A plays no chain to realign` on a fixed track, and stopping, pausing or playing from somewhere forgets it. It's not an undo step. The status line says `Track 01 realigns on the 1bar grid; Space a again takes it back`, then `Track 01's realign taken back`, and the transport shows `REALIGN` while any realign waits, from the engine's report. **Left out:** a per-section track reset, which a section's chain choice already covers by bringing a chain in; drawing where the next reset falls on the phrase view, which the playhead going back to the top already shows; and resets counted in the track's own steps, which would make `1bar` mean a different length at each speed. Added on 2026-11-09.
96. **A `List` fill goes round up to eight values typed into one `Values` row, in place of `From` and `To`, counting only the rows it changes, with `Run` giving each value that many rows and `.` keeping the list** (#97, M19's sequencer logic, built to the issue's proposed design). **Prior art:** Polyend's Fill tool, which `Where` already follows, has only `Constant`, `From-To` and `Random` (tracker-ce's `gPatternEdit.cpp`), and an Elektron's velocity locks are set step by step, so an accent of three values is typed by hand on both. A short list of values going round under the notes is what the Metropolix's mod lanes give with a length of their own (the M12 lanes design), live; this writes it once as plain values, as the panel does everything. Counting only the rows changed is `Alternate`'s rule, so a three-value accent stays in order however the hits are spaced, rather than falling on every third row. **The data:** `Shape.values`, a `Values` of a fixed `[u8; 8]` and a length, so a shape stays plain data that's `Copy`; eight is the issue's cap, and fits the panel's row as `70 40 55 ...` in 23 characters. **The panel:** `List` comes after `Alternate` in `Fill`'s turn and list, described `A few values in turn`. Its rows are `Fill`, `Values`, `Run` and `Where`, as `Curve` and `S` hide for a wave. `Values` is typing only, as the issue has it: `i` starts empty, as on every other row, two hex digits make a value and the space after it comes by itself, so `704055` types `70 40 55`, and a space ends a value of one digit sooner, as `7 ` is `07`. A ninth value isn't taken, `Backspace` takes back one character, `Enter` keeps it, `Esc` leaves the list as it was, and `Enter` on nothing is refused with the status line saying `Values are hex values like 70 40 55, up to 8 of them`. A value over the column's top is kept at the top, as typing `From` does. `=` and `-` on it say `Type the values with i`. **Choices the issue left open:** the list starts as `From` and `To`, the two ends the panel opened with or was set to, so switching from `Alternate` to `List` sounds the same until the list is typed, and `x` on `Values` puts that back; a list once typed stays while `Fill` moves through the others and back. `~`, which swaps the ends, turns the list round, as swapping the ends of an `Alternate` does, and the hint under the rows says `~ reverse the list`. `i` replacing the whole list, rather than starting with it to edit, was chosen to match the panel's other rows; with eight values at most it's a short retype. **Left for later,** as the issue has it: merging `Alternate` into `List`, and nudging one value with `=` and `-` by selecting within the row. Added on 2026-11-09.
97. **A sampler plays a span of its bank entry, compiled on the edit thread into a `Playable`: the entry's start, end and loop, in frames at its file's own rate, its gain, tune and cents, reverse and fades, cut by the track's `Start` (`STA`), `Length` (`SLEN`) and `Play` (`PLY`: one-shot, loop or ping-pong), with a declick wherever it cuts into the sound, and a point moved under a playing note heard at once** (#269, the second step of the sample editor's design, #268). **Prior art:** the Digitakt's `STRT`, `LEN`, `LOOP` and loop position, track parameters a step can lock and an LFO can move, with the length counted on its own rather than from the start; the Octatrack's trim and loop points in samples, kept per slot; the Polyend Tracker's start, end and two loop points with one-shot, forward, backward and ping-pong loops; the Deluge's markers in frames at the file's own rate and its loop crossfade into the margin before the loop's start, linear as its time stretcher's are, "less jarring" on short hops (`model/voice/voice_sample.cpp`). **The model:** `SampleEdit` gains `points: Points { start, end: Option, loop: Option<Loop { start, end, crossfade }> }`, all in the file's own frames, and `PlayParams` gains `cents`, `reverse`, `fade_in` and `fade_out`, the fades in the file's frames too. Every new field has a default and is left out of `song.ron` when it has it, so older songs open as they were and `FORMAT_VERSION` stays 6. **Whose is the mode:** the entry has the loop's place and the track has whether it loops, as a Digitakt's sample has its loop position and its track `LOOP`: the track's settings act on whichever entry plays (decision 94), so a lock can play one hit of a pad looped and the next once. An entry with no loop of its own loops all that plays. **`Start` and `Length`:** shares of the entry's span, `00` to `FF`, as the Digitakt's are, with `FF` all of it: the note starts `Start` of the way in and plays `Length` of the whole from there, stopping at the span's end. They count in the order it plays, so on a reversed entry they count back from its end. They're continuous, with a fine part, a step locks them and an LFO or an envelope moves them, read as each note starts, as `SMP` is. `PLY` is a list, `One-shot`, `Loop` and `Ping-pong`, which modulation can't move, as with `GRV` and `RATE`, since a way of playing is chosen rather than swept. The labels are `STA`, `SLEN` (the author's, as `LEN` is `Gate`'s, decision 78) and `PLY`, stored as `sampler.start`, `sampler.length` and `sampler.play`. Thru comes with slices (#270). **The `Playable`:** in `vt_model::playable`, so the engine never reads a `SampleEdit`: `Playable::of(entry, pool)` resolves the file, turns points into the pool's frames, clamps them to the file and the crossfade to the room before the loop and half its length, and gives an `Arc` of the frames and a table of spans, one for now, each a `Copy` `Span` with its start, end, loop, reverse, fades, gain and speed. `Published.bank` is an `Arc<[Option<Arc<Playable>>]>`; `Published::after` keeps the last version's whole table when the bank and pool are the same, and otherwise each entry's `Arc` when the entry is equal and its file the same `Arc`, so only a changed entry is worked out again, and the app keeps the version it last sent for this. **The file's rate:** the pool now keeps the rate each file was recorded at beside the rate it was converted to (`SamplePool::load`), so a point at a 44.1 kHz file's frame 4410 plays from the program's frame 4800 at 48 kHz. **The voice** reads in play order, frames counted from the file's first or, reversed, its last, so reversing is only how a place becomes a frame, and loops, fades and ends work the same either way. A start or an end that cuts into the sound fades over a declick, `DECLICK_FRAMES`, at least; at the file's own edges there's none, so a whole file plays bit for bit as before and both golden renders are unchanged. The fade in runs from the note's start, and the fade out to the end only for a one-shot, as a looping note never reaches its end. **Loops:** a forward loop's crossfade runs from before the loop's start, the Deluge's pre-margin, so it's over as the loop starts again. It's a linear crossfade, equal in gain, as a loop's two ends are usually alike and an equal-power one swells a steady sound by 3 dB. With none set it's a declick long, and with no room before the start, a loop from the file's first frame, it runs on past the end instead. The mix is made live by a second read head, only while it lasts, rather than baked into a tail when compiling: the loop `Start` and `Length` make differs from note to note, and the same head smooths a loop moved under a playing note. Ping-pong turns at either end, which is smooth already, so it has no crossfade. **Heard at once:** when a version arrives, each voice and waiting note playing an entry whose file is the same takes its new span from the frame it's on: one past its new end fades out over a declick, one past its loop goes round with a crossfade, a reversed one reads on from the same frame the other way, a new gain glides over about a millisecond, and a new tune moves its pitch. **Setting points:** until the sample page (#274), `:sample` takes a word: `start`, `end`, `loop from to [crossfade]` or `loop off`, `reverse` (`on`, `off` or neither to turn it over), `fade in out`, `gain dB` and `tune semitones [cents]`, with times as `1.5` seconds or `250ms`. Each changes the entry the current track's `Sample` is on, as one undo step, and the status line says what it is now. They're words of `:sample`, as #268 names `:sample default`, since `:loop` keeps the section. **The page:** `Start`, `Length` and `Play` sit under `Sample` on the Sound page, where a synth's settings are, so a sampler's page has a synth's shape: bars for the two shares, and the mode's name, which `K` lists. **Left out:** Thru, slices and per-slice settings (#270), snapping to zero crossings and normalising (#272), drawing the points on the Sound page's waveform, which the sample page does (#274), loop to tempo (#286), and a row for cents, which only `:sample tune` sets. Added on 2026-11-09.
98. **Each sample file has an analysis, kept by its content hash in the cache folder: a min/max peak pyramid, its peak level and where its sound is. `:normalize` is a gain worked out from it and `:trim` sets the entry's start and end from it, so neither writes audio, and `u` takes either back** (#272, the fourth step of the sample editor's design, #268). **Prior art:** Ableton's `.asd` keeps a sample's analysis beside it, which #268 rules out for us, as we never write next to the user's files; Audacity and REAPER keep peak summaries at several resolutions, Audacity's finest every 256 samples; Bitwig's Normalize writes a gain on the event rather than audio; tracker-ce's normaliser scales each channel by its own peak, which shifts a stereo sample's balance. **Where it's kept:** `vimtracker/analysis/<hash>`, the hash as 32 hex digits, in `$XDG_CACHE_HOME` if it's set, on any system, as the config folder follows `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, or else in `~/Library/Caches` on macOS and `~/.cache` elsewhere. Never in the song, never in the project, never beside a sample. **The file** is a small binary format of our own, since RON would be megabytes of text for a long file: `VTAN`, `ANALYSIS_VERSION`, the hash, rate, channels, length, peak, the sound's range, then each level's lowest and highest samples as little-endian `f32`s. One of another version, of another content, or cut short is built again and written over. It's written to a `.part` file and renamed, so a reader never sees half of one. **What's in it,** at the file's own rate and in its own channels, as an entry's points are: the pyramid, whose finest buckets are 256 frames, as Audacity's are, up to one bucket over the whole file, which is 1.8 MB for 5 minutes of stereo at 48 kHz (each halving of the finest bucket would double it, and a view closer than 256 frames a column reads the frames themselves, the sample page's business in #274); the peak, of whichever channel is louder; and the sound, from the first frame above -60 dBFS in any channel to just past the last. Onsets, the Deluge's perc measure and a tempo guess join it with #277 and #285, raising the version. **Reading it:** `fold` and `peak` over any stretch read at most two buckets a level, so O(log n), exact on the finest buckets' edges and otherwise covering the buckets either end is in; `view` reads each column from the level whose buckets are nearest its width without being wider, so O(columns) at any zoom. **Where it's built:** in the program, on a thread of its own, `Analyst`, which takes every file asked for in turn, unlike the explorer's loader, which reads only the newest. Each time the app publishes a version, it asks for every bank file with no analysis yet, so a file joining the bank, a project opening and an undo all ask without a call of their own. Analyses live in the `SamplePool`, by hash, so `Playable::of` finds a file's peak without a new argument, and `Published::after` works an entry out again when its peak has come in since the last version, rather than keeping the last version's `Playable`, and a device switch keeps them, as they don't depend on the rate. A hit in the cache is trusted by its hash. A miss reads and hashes the file, and keeps the analysis under the hash of what it read, so a file changed since it joined the bank never gets another content's analysis; that entry plays as it is, and opening the project has already said it changed. `vimtracker render`, `bench`, `:render` and `:render --stems` wait for every analysis, so a render never depends on how far work in the background had got. **Normalise:** `PlayParams.normalize`, a peak level in dBFS from -60 to 0. `:normalize -0.3` sets it, `:normalize` alone brings the peak to 0 dBFS, and `:normalize off` clears it. The gain is the level over the file's peak, the same for every channel, so a stereo sample keeps its balance, and the entry's own gain, a kit's for instance, comes on top, as the track's `Level` comes on top of both. It's worked out on the edit thread into the gain of the `Playable`'s span, in `Playable::of`, read as each note starts as before, so it never clicks. Until the analysis is in, and for a silent file, it's 1. **`:trim`:** sets the entry's start and end to the sound's, with no end when the sound runs to the file's end, waiting for the analysis if it's on its way. -60 dBFS keeps a one-shot's tail without its noise floor; tracker-ce's export trims at about -72 dBFS. **Points:** `:trim` sets the `start` and `end` of the `SampleEdit.points` that #269 brought (decision 97), in frames at the file's own rate, and leaves its loop where it was. The sampler plays them, cut further by the track's `STA` and `SLEN`, so a trimmed entry is heard only from where its sound starts to where it ends. **Neither writes a file:** both are one undo step through `SetBankEntry`, as `:bank clean` is, and the source is never touched. Neither changes the format version, as both settings are left out of `song.ron` until they're set. **Left out:** a size limit and eviction for the cache folder, as analyses are small and the render cache's limits (#281) can cover both; a level for `:trim`, as in `:trim -48`, which would need the frames rather than the cached scan; normalising a slice, from `peak` over its range, with #270; and showing the level on the Sound page, which waits for the sample page. **Measured** on an Apple M5, release build, 5 minutes of stereo at 48 kHz (`cargo run --release -p vt-model --example analysis_bench`): building the pyramid 55 to 80 ms; the whole analysis from a 16-bit WAV, with reading, hashing and decoding it, 165 to 215 ms; the cache file 1.8 MB, read back in under a millisecond; a view 2400 columns wide 4 to 6 µs at every zoom from the whole file to 73 ms, and 160 columns wide under 0.5 µs; the peak of nearly the whole file 30 ns. Added on 2026-11-09.
99. **In the sample explorer a kit opens like a folder to its sounds, a sound with variants or layers opens to its files, and the kit folders and Ableton Live's sample library are places** (#303, M22). **Prior art:** Ableton's browser unfolds a Live Set or a rack in place with its arrow, while `Enter` or a double-click loads the thing itself, and its Drum Racks are what the imported kits came from; Elektron's +Drive and the M8's browser are folders of files, which the explorer already follows. **The kit:** any `.ron` that `load_kit` reads is listed as a folder, named by the kit, such as `909 Core Kit`, after the real folders as a file is, with the kit's preview beside it as `Space 8` shows it; a project's `song.ron` doesn't read as a kit, so it isn't listed. **Its sounds** are the rows the kit in use lists, label first with the name beside it, and choosing one is choosing it from that group: the bank entry takes its label, name, gain and tune, and the track its label and choke group. A sound with no label now shows its name rather than a blank, in that group too. **Variants and layers,** after Ableton's arrow: a sound with either has an arrow, `Enter` chooses the sound itself, its main file, and `l` opens it to one plain list of its own file, `Main`, its variants, `Variant 1` on, and its velocity layers, `Layer 1 soft` to the hardest, such as `Layer 4 hard`, kept short so a long file's name fits beside it, each by its file's name with that beside it. Any of them is chosen in the sound's place, with the sound's label, name, gain, tune and choke group, so a variant is a different file under the same name; the bank list shows the file beside it. A layer that is the main file again, as Ableton's imports have it, is listed as the kit says. The tree knows a sound by its place under the kit's path, as `909 Core Kit.ron/0`, and a file by its place under the sound's, so `H`, `Backspace` and a search keep them as they keep files. **Audition and preview:** a kit's sound has a sample's preview and plays as the cursor moves onto it, with `A` and `P` as for files, the file as it is rather than with the kit's gain and tune, as files play; this holds for the kit in use's group too. **A missing file** is listed, its preview says why it can't be read, as any unreadable file's does, and choosing it leaves the status line saying so, as `:sample` does. **Places:** recent, the project, `kits` (the settings' kit folders), the settings' `samples`, then `library`, then here and home: the library is the largest, so it comes last of the program's own. `library` is the `Samples` folder, with `Loops`, `One Shots` and `Multisamples`, of the Live that `kits import-ableton` finds by default, the newest in `/Applications`; it's found once as the program starts, kept as `App::library`, and none in tests, rather than a new setting, which would only ever say where Live is. **Left for later:** `L` on a kit in the sample explorer to put it on tracks, as `Space 8` does; auditioning with a kit's gain and tune; layers playing by velocity; and a chosen kit sound noting its kit's folder as a recent place, as its pick doesn't know the kit. Added on 2026-11-09.
100. **The picker scrolls with a margin of three rows beyond the cursor, held on the `Picker` rather than worked out from the cursor, and has Vim's `zz`, `zt`, `zb`, `C-d` and `C-u`** (the author's report of 2026-11-09, no issue). Every `K` list, the explorer, the sample browser and the command line's list of matches shared a view that scrolled only once the cursor was on the last row, so going down a long list never showed what was below it. **Prior art:** Vim's popup menu, the command line's list's model, keeps up to 3 rows of context round the selected match (`pum_set_selected`'s "a few lines of context"); Vim's own `scrolloff` is 5 in `defaults.vim` and 0 in Neovim; snacks.nvim's picker takes the window's `scrolloff`, shrunk to half the list's height less one, which with the author's Neovim setting of 2 gives 2; Telescope has no margin, only `scroll_strategy`, and its `C-d` and `C-u` scroll the preview. **The margin:** 3, Vim's popup menu's number, about a fifth of the 16 rows a list shows and the 18 the explorer shows, and the same in the command line's list of 10. The author keeps Neovim's at 2 because a big `scrolloff` costs `C-e` and `C-y` runway, which the picker doesn't have, so the extra row is only more of what's coming. A list too short for it keeps half its rows less one, as snacks does, and the margin gives way at the list's ends, with no space after the last row. **State:** the first row shown is a field of the `Picker`, which moves only as far as the margin needs, so the view holds as the cursor turns round and after a `zz`; drawing records the rows the list had room for, which `zz` and `C-d` go by, and fits the view to that room, as ratatui's `ListState` keeps its offset. Searching, opening and closing a category or folder, `gg`, `G`, and the command line's Tab, which moves the cursor from outside, all fit it again rather than resetting it. **Keys:** `zz`, `zt` and `zb` put the cursor's row in the middle, at the top or at the bottom, keeping the margin as Vim does with `scrolloff`. Nothing shows before the first row, but `zz` and `zt` show space after the last, as Vim shows the space after a buffer's last line, so the last rows can sit in the middle or at the top; moving keeps that space until the margin needs the view back, and moving alone never makes it (the author found `zt` did nothing in a 22-row `K` list, which could only scroll 6 rows); `z` now waits for another key as `g` does, so a search for a name starting with `z` needs `/`. `C-d` and `C-u` move the cursor and the view half the list's rows, as Vim does, in the search too, as snacks binds them and as `C-n` and `C-p` work there; a count before them is ignored rather than setting the amount as Vim's `scroll` does. **`Esc`:** in the search it goes back to the list, keeping the search and what it found, as `Esc` leaves Insert mode and as snacks' picker leaves its input for its list, so `j`, `k` and `zz` move through the matches; only the list's own `Esc` closes. Before, it cleared the search, and closed a list that had none, so a list opened on its search, as the FX commands and tracks are, closed on the first `Esc` (the author's report of 2026-11-09). A list opened on its search now takes two. **Other lists:** the command line's list is the same picker and view, so it has the margin too. The chain view's slots, the song view and the guides scroll by their own code and weren't changed. Added on 2026-11-09.
101. **vimtracker requires the Nerd Font symbols, and uses them for icons and switches** (the author, reviewing #304). It follows from running only in kitty and Ghostty (decision 50): both ship the symbols built in, kitty as `Symbols Nerd Font Mono` and Ghostty embedded, as a fallback for whatever font is in use, so requiring them asks nothing more of anyone. **Prior art:** snacks.nvim's explorer, which the explorer follows, draws its file icons with them, as most Neovim pickers and file trees do. **Glyphs** live in named constants in `glyphs.rs`, each with its Nerd Font name and codepoint, such as `SWITCH_ON`, `nf-fa-toggle_on`, U+F205. **Icons carry meaning,** as a switch's state does, never decoration, in keeping with the spartan visuals, and colour goes with them only when it means something too. **The first use:** the explorer's hint shows `H`, `P` and `A` each with a switch, `nf-fa-toggle_on` in green while it's on and `nf-fa-toggle_off`, U+F204, faint while it's off, as `Enter choose  H  hidden  P  preview  A  audition`, in every explorer that has them: all have `H`, all but the folder choosers for `:w` and `:render` have `P`, and only the sample explorer has `A`. The hint was chosen over the box's title because the switch then sits beside the key that flips it and the word that names it, so nothing is said twice, and the hint is already the line about those keys. The toggle pair was chosen over an eye and a speaker, which would say what as well as whether, because the word beside it already says what, and the toggle's two states read apart at one cell where a slash through an eye barely does. The explorer's hints now part their keys with two spaces rather than three, and its list is 54 characters wide rather than 52, so the sample explorer's fits under its list with the preview off, the box at its narrowest. **Left for later:** icons for files, folders and kits in the explorer. Added on 2026-11-09.
102. **The sample page, `Space 0`, shows the cursor's track's sample as a waveform 161 characters by 28, drawn at one pixel for each of the screen's, with Vim's motions and zoom over it, a ruler of the points and the time, an overview of the whole sample, and the entry's points; it only shows the sample, and its cursor is a two-pixel line the program places at its frame, or its column's background, as `zc` switches** (#274, the sixth step of the sample editor's design, #268, M22). **Prior art:** tracker-ce's sample editor, read as prior art only: a waveform of 600 pixel columns, each its lowest and highest sample, with the start, end and loop as lines, its zoom a number of samples a pixel centred on the point last moved, and one encoder click one pixel at the zoom, and the author likes how big and pleasant it is; the Deluge's waveform view (`waveform_basic_navigator.cpp`), which keeps its scroll a multiple of its zoom, so columns sit on the zoom's grid, and draws the columns it had before a zoom while new ones come off the card; Audacity, which past a sample a pixel draws a line from sample to sample with a dot on each; Ableton's sample display, a ruler of times over the waveform; and Vim, whose `sidescroll` at its default of 0 brings a cursor that leaves the screen to its middle. **The layout,** for the author's screen, 163 by 42 in kitty, with a character 15 by 39 pixels: a header (the entry, its length, rate, channels and zoom), a gap, the ruler, the waveform, the overview, a gap, the entry's start, end and loop, and the cursor's time, frame and slice, 35 rows in all. The waveform is 28 rows rather than #268's 14: a stereo file is left above right, 14 rows each, which settles #268's sixth open point, and a mono one has all 28, so it's as big as the Polyend's against the screen. The page gives up the meter's column, so the waveform is the window's width less a column either side, 161 characters, 2415 by 1092 pixels. A wider window widens it; a smaller one shows the part of the page with the cursor's column in it, scrolling across, rather than squeezing it, as placeholders shown in part show that part of the image. **The drawing** (`sample_wave.rs`) is at the terminal's own character size in pixels, as `crossterm`'s `window_size` gives it: each pixel column from the pyramid (decision 98) down to 256 frames a pixel, its finest buckets, from the frames the pool holds below that, each joined to the column before so a fast wave stays joined up, and closer than a frame a pixel, a line two pixels thick from frame to frame with a five-pixel dot on each frame once frames are 6 pixels apart, so a zero crossing is where the line crosses each lane's faint zero line. Its colours are the theme's, each a meaning, as decision 93 has it: the sound that plays in the text's colour, sound before the start or after the end dimmer, zero faint, the start teal, the end red and the loop blue, each a line two pixels wide at its frame through every lane. The cursor is rosewater, the cursor's colour everywhere; the playhead (#275) stays green and the selection the selection's grey, as they are elsewhere. The ruler marks each point with `▾` in its colour over its column, and the time where there's room, faint, every 1, 2 or 5 of a power of ten of a second, and from 10 seconds in steps minutes divide into; the settings row names each point in its colour, so it's the waveform's key too. **Zoom** is frames a column: a power of two from 1, or the widest, as many as fit the whole sample across, which needn't be one, so `zO` fills the width. The view starts on a column's edge, as the Deluge's does. `zi` and `zo` halve and double it, from the widest to the power of two below it, keeping the cursor in its column on screen, so `zi` then `zo` comes back to the same view away from the ends. `M-1` and `M-2` zoom too, as they narrow and widen the grid elsewhere (decision 23), so #268's seventh open point is settled by having both. **Silky zoom** needs no drawing from the level above: the pyramid is in memory and reads O(columns) at any zoom, and the frames are in the pool, so each zoom is drawn from its own level at once. The waveform is sent again on a zoom, a scroll, a change of points or a new window size, and never for the cursor; the overview only for the points or the window. **Measured** with `cargo run --release -p vt-tui --example sample_page_cost` on a five-minute stereo file at 44.1 kHz, 13.2 million frames, in kitty 0.50.0 at 15 by 39 pixels a character, the median of nine: from the key to the screen written, through `Graphics::sync` and ratatui, 6 to 8 ms from the whole sample to 1024 frames a column and 3 to 5 ms closer, and kitty then decodes the picture in 13 to 20 ms, so 16 to 27 ms from the key to the image at every zoom, with 41 to 76 KB written, most at the middling zooms where a drum loop's columns are busiest. A scroll costs as a zoom does. Moving the cursor is 0.4 ms and 146 bytes as a line, and 2.6 KB as a column. **Motions,** each with a count: `h` `l` a column at the zoom shown; `0` `$` the slice's start and last frame; `^` `g_` its first and last frame above -60 dBFS, found in the pyramid's finest buckets and then the frames; `gg` `G` the sample's first and last frame, and with a count, a slice; `j` `k` the next and previous slice; `}` `{` the next and previous beat at the song's tempo on a grid from the entry's start; `]]` `[[` the next and previous point; `H` `M` `L` the view's left, middle and right, with `3H` the third column; `C-d` `C-u` half a screen, view and cursor; `C-e` `C-y` a column of view, pushing the cursor along at the edge; `zz` `zs` `ze` the view with the cursor in its middle, at its left or at its right, after Vim's horizontal `zs` and `ze`; and `zv` the slice, at the closest zoom that fits it. A cursor that leaves the view brings it half a screen along, as Vim's `sidescroll` of 0 does, so holding `l` sends the waveform every half screen rather than every column. **`{n}G` counts in decimal,** naming a slice by its number from `00` as the ruler and `SLI` will show it in hex, so `3G` is slice `03`, which settles #268's fifth open point: hex digits are keys in Normal mode, and slice 0 is `gg`. **Until slices come** (#270), the entry from its start to its end is one slice, `00`: `0` and `$` go to its ends, `j`, `k` and `{n}G` to its start, and `]]` and `[[` go between the start, the end and the loop's start and end, which slices join. **Ways in:** `Space 0`, and `g d` on the Sound page's `Sample` row, both jumps `C-o` comes back from; `W` and `B` show the next or previous track's sample, each starting with the whole sample in view and the cursor on its start, and coming back to one keeps where the page was only for the sample last shown. **The cursor's line,** after the author's comment on #274: a cell background is a character wide, about 15 pixels, so the cursor is also an image of its own, a two-pixel line in its colour, sent once with `a=t` and placed with `a=p` at its character, `X=` pixels into it, so moving it is placing it again, 146 bytes. It's placed after each frame is drawn, as ratatui clears the screen when the window changes size and a clear frees an image that isn't shown through placeholders, in kitty 0.50.0 and Ghostty 1.4.0 alike, so it's sent again then; and taken off while anything is drawn over the page, as a placed image shows through a window drawn over it where a placeholder image doesn't. It's the default, and `zc` switches to the column's background, so the author can choose by eye; the playhead (#275) can follow whichever is chosen. **Left for later:** slice numbers and marks in the ruler (`03▾a`), `]]` and `[[` by slice, and `j`, `k` and `{n}G` over real slices (#270); `'a`, `]'` and `['` (#271); `g d` on a step's `SLI` value, which needs `SLI` (#270); the playhead and auditioning (#275); the settings row's snap (#276), sensitivity (#277) and slice count (#270), and working on it with `=`, `-` and `K` (#276); Visual mode and the selection, in the status row too (#276 and the operators after it). Added on 2026-11-09.
103. **Every row of the explorer has a one-cell Nerd Font icon that says what it is, in place of the folders' arrows, and every window has rounded corners** (#306, and the first item of #308; the author's direction of 2026-11-09: polished with restraint, Ableton-like but proud to be a console program, every glyph carrying meaning, colour only where it means something). **Prior art:** snacks.nvim's explorer draws `nf-md-folder` and `nf-md-folder_open` with no arrows, and the same Material Design set for files; nvim-web-devicons, yazi and lf give audio files one music icon; Ableton's browser shows a sample as a waveform and a Drum Rack as a grid of pads. **The icons**, each a constant in `glyphs.rs` under `// Explorer`, all from Material Design so they read as one set: a folder closed or open, `nf-md-folder` U+F024B and `nf-md-folder_open` U+F0770, which say what the arrow did; a project, `nf-md-folder_music` U+F1359; a kit, as a grid of pads, `nf-md-view_grid` U+F0570, chosen over `nf-fa-drum`, which decision 104 gives a drum voice's track, and over a box, which says software package; one of a kit's sounds as one pad, `nf-md-square_rounded` U+F14FB, and a sound with variants or layers, which opens, as pads stacked, `nf-md-checkbox_multiple_blank` U+F0136; a sample, `nf-md-waveform` U+F147D, as Ableton's; a loop, `nf-md-repeat` U+F0456; and a file gone or unreadable, `nf-md-alert` U+F0026. **Places** keep the folder's icon and the dim word beside them, `recent`, `kits`, `library`, `here` and the rest, since an icon of their own would only say the word again. **Loops** are told from one-shots by name alone, so it costs nothing and needs no file read: the nearest folder whose name has the word `loop` or `loops` makes a loop, and one with `one shot`, `one shots` or `oneshots` a one-shot, as Ableton's library has `Loops` and `One Shots`, whose one-shots can have a tempo in their names, such as `Impact Deep Pad 120 bpm.wav`; failing those, a tempo of 40 to 300 before `bpm` in the name, as sample packs name loops, makes one. Anything else is a sample: a single icon covers one-shots and the unknown. **Missing and unreadable:** a kit's sound, variant or layer whose file isn't there is known as the kit is opened, a folder that can't be listed as it's opened, and a sample that won't decode once the preview or the audition has tried it, which marks it wherever it's listed; each shows the warning in the error colour. **Colour:** icons are dim, the chosen one, the track's sample, the kit in use in `Space 8` or the open project, is green, as what's current in a list is, in place of the `•` the tree had, and a warning is red; the kit in use at the top of the sample explorer is not coloured, as its heading already names it. **Width:** kitty and Ghostty widen a symbol over a plain space after it, which kitty's own renderer showed for nearly every icon here, so each is followed by a no-break space, U+00A0, `glyphs::ICON_SPACE`, which neither widens over, and the `H`, `P` and `A` switches now are too; the explorer's rows and widths are unchanged, as the icon and its space take the two cells the arrow and its space did. **Corners:** `views::window_border` gives ratatui's `BorderType::Rounded`, `╭╮╰╯`, which both terminals draw themselves; the picker, the explorer, the command line's list of matches, the guides, the keys, the shape panel and the box round the track page while its keyboard plays all use it. The boxes inside a page, round the filter curve and the waveform and round the phrase in Insert, stay square, as they're part of the grid rather than windows over it. **Screenshots** name Nerd Fonts after the ordinary monospace ones, so a browser with one installed shows the icons, and a new one, `explorer.svg`, shows the sample explorer. Added on 2026-11-09.
104. **Glyphs stand for the transport, a track's machine or kind, its mute and solo, an LFO's wave, a value's lock, dropouts and background work, each in place of a word or beside the word it adds to** (#308, items 2 to 6). **Prior art:** Ableton's transport is play, stop and record buttons with no words, its record lit red, and a clip waiting for its launch quantisation flashes; its status bar shows `Analyzing` while it works on a file, at the right, and its CPU meter lights an overload. Elektron's LFO page draws each wave, and its mixer's mute is a lit key. **The transport** is `nf-md-play` (U+F040A) in the active colour while playing, `nf-md-pause` (U+F03E4) or `nf-md-stop` (U+F04DB) dim, and `nf-md-record` (U+F044A) after play while recording, red as every record button is, rather than in the active colour, which would read as play. These replace `playing`, `paused`, `stopped` and `REC`, so nothing is said twice. `FILL` stays a word. A realign or a cued launch waiting for the launch grid is `nf-md-timer_sand` (U+F051F), dim, before `REALIGN` or the new `LAUNCH`, which the status line didn't show before. **Dropouts:** once audio has dropped out, the right of the status line shows `nf-md-alert` (U+F0026) and the count of late blocks and xruns together, in the warning colour, until the device is opened again, as the top right's `late` and `xruns` count. Nothing shows while there are none. **Background work:** while the analyst or the explorer's loader is working, and once the work has gone on for 300 ms, so a file read as the explorer's cursor passes doesn't flash, the right of the status line shows Fira Code's spinner, which the Nerd Font symbols carry unnamed at U+EE06 to U+EE0B, turning every 100 ms, and `Analysing` or `Loading`, dim. It's drawn only while there's work, so an idle screen's frames are the same and ratatui sends nothing. A render and `:kit` load on the main thread, which holds the screen, so no spinner can turn for them; they'd need to move off it first, which is left for later. **LFO shapes** are Material's waves, one cell, after the shape's name, in place of the 4-cell images: `nf-md-sine_wave` (U+F095B) for `Sine`, `nf-md-triangle_wave` (U+F147C) for `Tri`, `nf-md-square_wave` (U+F147B) for `Square`, and `nf-md-sawtooth_wave` (U+F147A) for `Ramp`, as Material's saw rises. `Saw`, which falls, `Random` and `Smooth` have no glyph and keep their name alone, rather than the nearest glyph, as a rising saw beside `Saw` or a die beside `Random` would say something untrue or only restate the name. The glyph is dim, and in the active colour while the cursor is on that LFO, as the images were. **A track's machine or kind** goes before its heading on the track page and in the phrase view, dim, and before each machine in the machine list: `nf-md-waveform` (U+F147D) a sampler, `nf-md-piano` (U+F067D) a synth voice, `nf-fa-drum` (U+EE32) `Kick`, `Snare` and `Hat`, `nf-md-arrow_u_left_top` (U+F17B3) a return, where the sends come back, and `nf-md-speaker` (U+F04C3) the master, which is what's heard. **Mute and solo** are `nf-md-volume_mute` (U+F075F) and `nf-md-headphones` (U+F02CB), in the active colour, after the heading's track name and in the last cell of the track's label over its column in the phrase view's overview, in place of `m` and `s`, and on the mixer's `Mute` and `Solo` rows in place of `M` and `S`. Headphones for solo is the convention of mixers with a solo per channel. **A lock:** a value's `Lock` mark in the phrase grid, the one cell after its value, is `nf-md-lock` (U+F033E), dim, in place of `.`, as it is the mark named lock; `h` and `g` stay letters. It is the only glyph in the grid. **Width:** each glyph is one cell to ratatui and to the terminal, so no column moves, which the live check confirmed in kitty with `get-text`. kitty and Ghostty draw a wide icon over a space after it, so every glyph has a space or an empty cell after it, and all draw the same way, apart from the lock in the last FX column in Insert, which the box's edge follows. **Screenshots** name `Symbols Nerd Font Mono` after the monospace fonts. **Not here:** a metronome or other icon that only restates a label, powerline pills, beat lines and finer curves (#308's items 7 and 8). Added on 2026-11-09.
105. **A bank entry's slices are numbered by where they start, as Vim numbers lines, each with an id of its own that's never used again and settings of its own that are otherwise the entry's; a sampler's `Slice`, `SLI` on a step, chooses which plays, clamped to the last, `PLY`'s `Thru` plays on to the entry's end, `:slice 16` and `:slice clear` cut and uncut it as one undo step that moves the steps' `SLI` locks with their sound, and the sample page's ruler, `j`, `k`, `{n}G`, `]]` and `[[` go by real slices** (#270, the third step of the sample editor's design, #268, M22). **Prior art:** Renoise's slices, markers that each play as a sample of their own with their own volume, panning and loop, a new one inheriting the original's; tracker-ce's slicer, read as prior art only, whose slices are starts only, a slice ending at the next, adding one renumbering the rest, `Evenly` cutting equal ones and `Play Thru` playing from a slice to the sample's end (`play_memory_slice.cpp`); Simpler's `Thru`; the Digitakt's and the Octatrack's slice as a lockable parameter an LFO can move; and the Deluge, whose slicer makes a kit row per slice and keeps no list, which ours keeps. **The model:** `Points` gains `slices: Vec<Slice { id, start, over: SliceParams }>`, in order of their starts, and `next_id`, which only grows, so a mark (#271) can name a slice through any renumbering. A slice ends at the next one's start or the entry's end, inside the entry's start and end; with none, the entry is one slice, `00`. At most 256, as `SLI`'s two digits number them. `SliceParams` holds a gain, a pan, a tune, a reverse and the two fades, each `None` for the entry's own, and the entry's cents and normalising still apply. The fields are optional with serde defaults, so `FORMAT_VERSION` stays 6. **Pan** is new to a sample: -50 to 50, as Ableton's and Renoise's go from 50 left to 50 right, at equal power as the track's pan is, and exactly 1 for both channels in the middle, so every golden render is unchanged; the voice glides it over the same millisecond a new gain takes. **Compiled away:** `Playable` now has a span for each slice, worked out on the edit thread with the slice's settings in place of the entry's, so a slice's settings cost the voice nothing, and the entry's start and end beside them for `Thru`. A slice keeps the entry's loop only if the loop is inside it, and otherwise loops all of itself, as an entry with no loop does. **`SLI`:** the sampler's second setting, `sampler.slice`, a list of `00` to `FF` read as each note starts, as `SMP` is, so a lock plays another slice for one note and an LFO sweeps through them a slice for each step of depth. Past the last slice it plays the last, and an entry with none ignores it. A voice keeps its slice number, so moving a point under it is heard as before. **`Thru`** is `PLY`'s fourth choice, after Ping-pong: once, from the slice's start on past its end to the entry's, and, reversed, back past the slice's start to the entry's, so it's the slice's way of playing carried on rather than a loop. `STA` and `SLEN` are shares of what plays, the slice or the slice carried on. **`:slice 16`** cuts the entry from its start to its end into that many of the same length, in decimal or `0x10`, as Polyend's `Evenly` and Renoise's equal slicing do, and `:slice clear` takes them away; each is one `Action::SetSlices`. A new slice that starts where an old one did keeps its id and settings, so `:slice 16` twice changes nothing and `:slice 8` after it keeps every other slice as it was. **Renumbering:** a re-slice from slices to slices moves each step's `SLI` lock to the new slice holding the frame the old one started on, as #271's marks will move, in the same undo step. A step plays the entry its own `SMP` locks, or else its track's `Sample`, and only steps playing the entry change; a number past the last follows the last, and an LFO's depth is a distance, so it's left. Slicing for the first time, or clearing, leaves the locks alone, as there's no numbering to follow: so `SLI 00` to `0F` written down a phrase before `:slice 16` plays the loop in order once it's sliced, and a lock cleared away comes back with the slices. **A slice's own settings,** until the sample page edits them (#276): `:slice gain -3`, `pan -25`, `tune 7`, `reverse on` or `off`, and `fade 10ms 0.2`, each alone to play as the entry again, on the slice the sample page's cursor is in, or off the page the one the track's `Slice` is on. **`K` on `SLI`,** on a step or the Sound page's `Slice` row, lists the slices of the sample it plays as `03  0.214 s`, each with how long it is, as #268 has it, with room for #271's marks between. The status line says which slice a step's `SLI` plays, of how many, from where and for how long, and the Sound page shows `of 16 at 0.484 s` beside `Slice`, or `whole`. **The Sound page** has `Slice` under `Sample`, so a sampler's five settings run down the left as an eight-setting synth's do, with `Groove` beside `Play` and the filter one row lower, and the output column where it was. **The sample page:** each slice's start is a line one pixel wide in peach, under the points' lines where they meet, as there can be many; the ruler marks it with `▾` and its number in hex before it, `03▾`, where there's room, or after it at the left edge, which #268's `03▾a` leaves room for; `j`, `k`, `{n}G` and `gg` with a count go by slice, `0` and `$` to its ends, and `]]` and `[[` stop at every slice's start as well as the points; the settings row counts the slices, and the cursor's row names its slice in peach with the slice's own settings. **`g d` on a step's `SLI`** opens the page on that slice of the sample the step plays, its own `SMP`'s if it has one, as a jump `C-o` comes back from; `Space 0` shows the track's own sample again. **Left for later:** marks and `SLI 'a` (#271), splitting, joining and nudging slices on the page and setting their own settings there (#276), auditioning a slice (#275), slicing by transients, beats and silence (#277), and a note-to-slice mode, as #268's tenth open point has it. Added on 2026-11-09.
106. **The phrase grid marks where each beat and bar begins with a rule under the row before it, an underline in a colour of its own, brighter for a bar than a beat and none between steps; no curve moves to octants or sextants** (#308, items 7 and 8; the author's direction of 2026-11-09). **Prior art:** Ableton's clip grid draws a bar's line darker than a beat's and a sixteenth's faintest, and Renoise bands its beat rows; M8 and the Polyend Tracker light the beat's row number, as the yellow numbers here already do. **The rule** is ratatui's underline with an underline colour, SGR 4 and 58, under every cell of the row's steps and FX, the cursor's and a selection's included, so it reads as one line across the grid: `SURFACE1` before a beat, a shade quieter than an empty cell's dashes, and `OVERLAY0`, as bright as those dashes, before a bar. It goes under the row before because that's where a line between the two can go: a rule of its own would take a row, a quarter of the screen at a beat every four, and nothing else moves. The beat and bar are those of the row's start in time, so a row after rows lasting several pulses gets the rule it starts on, as its yellow number does. The loop's last row has a bar's rule, as the loop starts again after it, and the row before the loop has one too; the phrase's last row has none, as nothing follows. Rows outside the loop count by their numbers. The overview's other tracks and the gutter have no rule, as their rows may fall differently. **Tried and dropped:** a ruler in the gutter's last cell, `│` with `├` before a beat and `┝` before a bar, and short ticks, `╶` and `╺`, alone. Each touched the note beside it, so a tick read as a minus sign before `C-4`, and marked the beat's row as the yellow number does rather than the line between beats. kitty and Ghostty both have styled underlines, dashed or double, but ratatui writes only a plain one, so a bar is told by brightness rather than weight. **Finer curves:** kitty 0.50 and Ghostty 1.4 both draw sextants (U+1FB00 on) and octants (U+1CD00 on) themselves, as `docs/terminal-notes.md` records, but no curve is drawn in text: the envelopes, the filter, the EQ, the compressor, the scope and the waveforms are kitty images on 6×12 dots a cell, or the screen's pixels on the sample page, and the meters' eighth blocks are finer up and down than an octant's quarters. So nothing changes, and the images stay. **Screenshots** draw an underline as a one-pixel line two pixels under the text, in its colour. Added on 2026-11-09.
107. **The sample page's `h` and `l` move its cursor's line a pixel, and held, speed up to a column a repeat over half a second** (the author, trying decision 102's page: the line moved a character at a time, about 15 pixels, and didn't feel smooth). This replaces decision 102's `h` `l` a column. **Prior art:** tracker-ce's sample editor moves a pixel at the zoom for each encoder click; the Polyend Tracker's and the Deluge's encoders take bigger steps the faster they're turned, so a slow turn places finely and a fast one crosses the sample. **A pixel** is of the character's width the pictures are drawn for, which `Graphics` gives the page each frame. The cursor goes to the first frame of the pixel one along, so every press moves the line exactly a pixel, about `zoom / width` frames, wherever in its pixel the cursor was, and the line is placed at the pixel its frame is in, as before. Closer than a frame a pixel, it moves a frame. A count moves that many pixels, or frames closer in, and never speeds up. As its column's background (`zc`), or with no pictures drawn, as in a render, the cursor can only show columns, so it moves a column, as before. **Holding:** a repeat, or a press of the same key within 60 ms of the last step, since kitty on macOS has sent a held key's repeats as presses, carries a run on. Each step in a run is `1 + (width - 1) × min(1, t / 0.5 s)²` pixels, `t` from the run's first repeat, so it's a pixel a repeat at first and a column, 15 pixels on the author's screen, half a second in; at a 30 ms repeat that's 1, 1, 1, 1, 2, 2, 3, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 10, 11, 12, 14 and then 15, about 7 columns before it's at full speed. A square's curve rather than a straight one keeps the first repeats a pixel, for placing, and closer in, where a column is fewer frames than the character's pixels, it still builds to a column. Any other key, the other direction, a count, or 250 ms without a repeat starts the next step from a pixel again. Added on 2026-11-09.
108. **The settings pages are laid out as a grid of fixed columns, ratatui `Table`s for their text and `Layout` cells for their images, with a `ValueBar` widget for a value's bar, so nothing counts characters to line its columns up** (#315; the author, after `Groove  Straight` among the bars, clarified that "tables" meant aligning the pieces, with no lines between cells). **Prior art:** M8's and the Elektron boxes' parameter pages, where every value sits in a fixed slot and a long name is cut rather than pushing its neighbours; ratatui's own `Table` with column `Constraint`s and `Cell::column_span`, and `Layout` for areas that hold widgets. **The grid:** `views::table::Columns` holds a page's column widths and the room between them, gives a `Table` its `Constraint`s, and gives `Layout` rects for the same columns. A row's cells go in from the left, each across one column or more, and a value too long for its cell is cut with `…`. There are no borders or separators, only the columns' widths and spacing, so the pages look as they did. With less room than its columns need, a table is cut at the right edge, as the paragraphs were, rather than its columns squeezed. **The Sound page,** in each half: name, value, fine part, bar, the room after the bar, and a gap. A word such as `Sampler`, `12 dB` or `Straight` spans from the value to the room after the bar, as a sample's file name needs. A choice's name, such as a 6-op patch's `E.PIANO 1`, `Saw` or a slice's `whole`, takes the bar's cell, so it starts where the bars do, two cells further right than before. Typed text with its `_` stays in the value's cell, so the fine part and the bar stay where they are from the first key, where the bar used to step a cell left. **The Mod page:** a table for each group, the envelopes, the LFOs, the accumulator and the arpeggio, with a space between columns. An LFO's shape is two columns, its name and its icon. The arpeggio's `Mode` is three cells wider, room for `Up-down-inc`, which ran into `Oct` before. **The FX page:** a slot's number, its effect, and for each column of settings a name, a value and a bar, a choice spanning the value's and the bar's cells. The gap before a slot's drawing is a column of its own, taken from the fifth setting's name. **The project page, the audio page and the mixer** are tables too. The groove's steps are a column each, right-aligned, and a mixer strip's name is cut with `…` rather than pushing the strips after it. **Images:** the filter's curve and the waveform, the envelopes' diagrams and the FX slots' drawings are drawn into `Layout` cells of the same columns, at exactly the cells they took before, so the kitty placements don't move. **`ValueBar`** keeps everything the bars showed: eighth-block fill, a mark either side of a centre for pan and pitch, a dim mark for what's set under a fill for what's playing while something moves it, and faint end caps. It becomes a styled `Line` for a table's cell and is also a `Widget`, drawn into any `Rect`. **The cursor's highlight** stays on the value's text, as before, rather than filling its cell, so a word in a wide cell isn't a long block. Added on 2026-11-09.
109. **The sample page's ruler marks are small triangles the terminal places at their lines' pixels, each kind of point an image of its own placed once for each of its points in view, with the slices' numbers and the times staying text beside them** (#316, M22; the author noticed on 2026-11-09 that a `▾`, snapped to the character its line is in, sits up to a character, about 15 pixels, off the line). This replaces decision 102's `▾` in the ruler where the pictures are drawn. **Prior art:** tracker-ce's sample editor draws each point's mark as a chevron joined to its line, in the same pixels, so a mark is never off its line (`points.cpp`, `drawPoint`); Ableton's sample display and Renoise's sample editor put each marker's flag on its line above the waveform; and the sample page's own cursor line (decisions 102 and 107), which this follows. **The mark** (`kitty::mark_png`) is a triangle pointing down at the bottom of a character, in its point's colour, its tip as wide as its line, 2 pixels for the start, end and loop and 1 for a slice, reaching a third of a character either side of it, 5 pixels on the author's screen, and a row taller than that, so its sides slope as `▾`'s do and its tip sits on the top of its line. There's one image for each kind, start, end, loop and slice, stored with `a=t` the first time the page is shown, and each point in view is a placement of its kind's image at its character, `X=` pixels into it, under a placement id of its own: 1 for the start, the end and the loop's start, 2 for the loop's end, and a slice's id and one, so a slice's mark keeps its placement as slices before it come and go. A mark is placed only when its line is in view, as the waveform has it. **What's sent:** only the placements that changed, after each frame is drawn, before the cursor's line. Moving the cursor sends nothing for the marks; a scroll or a zoom places each mark that moved and takes off each that left; moving a point places its mark. A scroll a column along is about 56 bytes for each mark in view, measured in tests at 15 by 39 pixels a character: 391 bytes for the 7 in view at 256 frames a column with 16 slices, and about 1.7 KB for the 30 in view at 64 frames a column with 256 slices, against 41 to 76 KB for the waveform the scroll sends; in kitty 0.50.0 with `:slice 16` on Ableton's `Breakbeat 134 bpm.aif` at 512 frames a column, 224 bytes for the 4 in view. **As the cursor's line:** they're stored again, with new ids, when the window changes size, as the clear that comes with it frees an image placed directly, and they're taken off while anything is drawn over the page, a command for each kind rather than each mark. Meanwhile the ruler draws them as `▾` over their lines' columns, so the points stay shown at a character's accuracy, as they are in a render, which has no pictures. With `zc`'s cell cursor they stay placed. **The numbers and the times** are text, as before, kept clear of every column a mark covers, which can be the column either side of its line's when its line is near a column's edge: a slice's number goes in the two columns before the first, or after the last at the ruler's left edge. **Choices the issue left open:** the mark's size, a third of a character either side, close to `▾`'s; the tip on the line, touching the waveform, rather than a gap above it; and the ruler's own `▾` under an open window rather than nothing, which can leave a column between a slice's number and its mark there. Added on 2026-11-09.
110. **`vimtracker probe <spec> <out.wav>` plays one voice or one effect with no song, offline, from a TOML spec that `tools/compare/` also renders through a reference plugin hosted by Spotify's `pedalboard`, so a comparison is written once and rendered twice** (#186, its second step; the load check of 2026-11-10 settled `pedalboard` 0.9.26 as the host). **Prior art:** DDMF's Plugin Doctor measures a plugin by what comes out for a sweep, stepped levels or a burst, which is how the comparison works: outputs only, never a plugin's insides. **The spec** is TOML rather than RON, as the plan first had it, because the reference side is Python, whose standard library has read TOML since 3.11 with `tomllib`, and RON has no reader there; JSON has no comments, and a spec wants its reasons written beside its numbers. Rust reads it with the `toml` crate, pinned as every dependency is. A spec has `[ours]`, the machine, the effects in the track's slots and their settings, `[reference]`, the plugin's path, its parameters by `pedalboard`'s names and the `buffer_size`, and between them what both play, `notes` for a voice or `input` for an effect, at a `rate` for a `duration`. **Settings** use the keys songs store and `SettingId::from_key` reads, `acid.cutoff`, `1.comp.threshold`, or the track's own `cut`, `res`, `mode`, `pan`, `vol`, `pitch` and `glide`, with the values the program shows, `00` to `FF` as TOML's `0x40`, or a choice by its name; TOML's bare dotted keys make tables, which are flattened back into keys, so `acid.cutoff = 0x40` needs no quotes. A key the program doesn't have is refused with the keys beside it, as `1.comp.thresh` lists the compressor's; so is one the spec has no place for, another voice's or an effect's not in the slots, an LFO's, and one that only works on steps, such as `groove`. **Cases:** a spec can have `[[cases]]`, each changing some of either side's settings, rendered one at a time with `--case`, so five cutoffs are one spec. **Notes** play as held keys do, through `Engine::hold_preview` and `release_preview` at their exact frames, so nothing is sequenced and the track's voice, filter and effects are heard as they are: times and lengths in seconds, a MIDI note number, a velocity, an optional `accent` that raises the velocity to `0x70` or keeps it under, and `slide`, which holds a note on into the next, as the 303 and decision 46 have a slide belong to the note that glides on. **Input** is a WAV file, or a sine, Farina's exponential sweep, a sine at stepped levels, a tone burst, or silence, each made in double precision by the same formulas in Rust and Python, and `run.py` checks the two agree (they differ by 0). It goes through the track by the new `Engine::play_input`, a one-off file at full level with no fade in or out, rather than `play_file`'s preview level and 1 ms attack, so an effect's input is exactly what the spec says. The limiter is off, so nothing measured is clipped. **The reference side** always passes a host's buffer size, 512 unless the spec says, as Arturia's plugins put out silence at `pedalboard`'s default of 8192; a plugin is loaded once for all of a spec's cases, and each case starts from the values its parameters loaded with. Plugins write their own files as they load, which the README lists (Arturia's preset database and preferences, u-he's cache); nothing here writes in a plugin's folder or its preferences. **The first comparison** `run.py` prints is each side's peak, RMS and spectral centroid, the magnitude response in octave bands for a sweep, and the static curve for stepped levels. **Left for later:** plots and an HTML report, knob sweeps and fitting mappings to them, and the 303's accent and slide set, each its own PR. Added on 2026-11-10.
111. **Letters name slices, as Vim's marks name lines, and follow them through a split, a join and a re-slice in the same undo step; a step locks a slice by its letter, `SLI 'a`, resolved as each note starts against the entry it plays, and a letter naming nothing plays the track's own `Slice`; `:mark`, `:marks` and `:delmarks` name, list and remove them, and the sample page's `'a`, `]'` and `['` go to them as jumps** (#271, the fourth step of the sample editor's design, #268, M22; its third open point). **Prior art:** Vim's marks, which name lines, move with them as lines before them come and go, and go with a deleted line, `J` moving the joined line's marks onto the line it joins, with `'a`, `]'`, `['`, `:marks` and `:delmarks a-c` and `:delmarks!` as here, and `CTRL-A` moving a letter on with `nrformats` taking `alpha`; Renoise's slices, each a sample of its own that can be named; and the Digitakt's and Octatrack's slice lock, which is by number only and so plays another sound once a sample is sliced again, which a letter is for. **The model:** `Points` gains `marks: BTreeMap<char, SliceId>`, `a` to `z`, a serde default, so `FORMAT_VERSION` stays 6. One letter names one slice, and a slice can have several. `Points::reslice` moves each letter to the new slice holding its old slice's start, which is every follow rule at once: a split keeps the start, and so the id and the letters, on the part before (#268's rule), a join leaves the start of the slice taken in inside the one left, which so gains its letters, and `:slice clear` leaves no slice, so the letters go. A letter on a slice that's gone some other way names nothing and is dropped at the next re-slice. **Split and join** are `Action::SplitSlice { number, at }` and `Action::JoinSlices { number, slice }`, each one `SetSlices`-style change with the steps' number locks following their sound, with no keys yet: #276 binds `Enter` and `J` to them. **`SLI 'a`:** the slot's command is `SLI` and its fine byte, which a list never has, holds the letter, with the coarse byte `00`; a song stores it as `sampler.slice:'a`, with `h` for a hold, and the grid shows `'a` in the value's two columns. Renumbering leaves it, as it needs no rewriting. Each entry's `Playable` gets the slice number of each of the 26 letters, worked out on the edit thread, so the callback looks one up as a note starts, against the entry that note plays, its `SMP` or an LFO's, with an LFO's depth on `SLI` adding to it as to a number. **A letter naming nothing** (the open point; proposed in #268 and taken): the note plays the track's own `Slice`, the held `SLI` under the lock, or the track's setting if what's held is a letter too, so a pattern whose letter was removed falls back to what the track plays without the lock rather than to `00` or silence. The step keeps its letter, shown as it was, so naming a slice with it again brings the chop back, and the status line on it says `'a names no slice of 01 Breakbeat, so the track's own plays: 00 of 16`. **Editing one:** `=` and `-` move it through the letters, stopping at `a` and `z`; two hex digits typed over it lock by number again. **Commands,** before the page edits marks (#276), each one undo step on the entry: `:mark a` names the slice the sample page's cursor is in, or off the page the one the track's `Slice` is on, and `:mark a 03` a slice by its number in hex, as `SLI` and the ruler show it; a letter on another slice moves, and the status says from where. `:marks` lists the letters in the order of their slices, `'a  03  0.214 s`, in a picker whose `Enter` goes to the slice on the sample page, as a jump. `:delmarks` takes letters as Vim's does, `a b`, `abc` or `a-c`, and `:delmarks!` takes every one. While the sample page is the view they act on the entry it shows, and elsewhere on the track's sample. A sample with no slices has none to name, so `:mark` says to slice it first. **`K` on a step's `SLI`** lists each letter first, `'a  03  0.214 s`, to lock by, and then the slices, `03  a   0.214 s`, the letters in a column as wide as the most any slice has; the Sound page's `Slice` lists the slices with their letters, as a track's setting is a number. **The sample page:** the ruler puts a slice's letters after its mark, `03▾a`, in mauve, a colour of their own as the letters aren't points, kept clear of the columns a placed mark covers and given way to by the next slice's number; the settings row lists `Marks` in slice order and the cursor's row its slice's, as `slice 03  'a 'b`. `'` waits for a letter, as `"` does for a register, and `'a` goes to the start of the slice it names; `]'` and `['` go to the next or previous slice with a letter, by slice and with a count. All three are jumps: the jump list now keeps the sample page's cursor and the entry it showed, so `C-o` comes back to the frame it left, on the page or off it. **Left for later:** `m{a-z}` on the page, `d'a` and the other operators to a mark, `:'a,'b` ranges, `''` (#276 and the operators after it), and a timeline cut dropping the letters on slices wholly inside it (#280). Added on 2026-11-10.
113. **`tools/compare/report.py` writes one self-contained page for each comparison spec, both sides plotted together by matplotlib as embedded PNGs with the measurements in tables, and the first real measurements are filter sweeps, self-oscillation, the Acid's filter and cutoff knob, and compressors' static curves, attack and release** (#186, its third step, after decision 110). **Prior art:** DDMF's Plugin Doctor again, whose views are the ones measured here, a sweep's magnitude response, a static curve and a burst's gain over time; Farina's sweep for the response, read as the output's spectrum over the input's in each FFT bin, a fraction of a hertz apart, so a resonance's peak is read whole. **The page** says what the test is, in a paragraph the spec carries as `[report] about`, what each line is, what differs most, worked out from the numbers, the plots and the tables, so it reads without our code; a spec's `[report]` names its kind, `response`, `harmonics`, `oscillation`, `static`, `timing` or `knob`, and lays its cases out by our settings, or the input's fields as `input.level`. Ours is blue and the reference orange on every plot. Pages are 0.1 to 2 MB, so they stay in the ignored `out/` and their numbers go in the PR. **The probe** gains four things, each tested and as deterministic as before: `filter` sets the track's filter type by the name the Sound page shows, as a song keeps it on the track rather than as a setting; an `impulse` input, one sample then silence, as a filter that self-oscillates has nothing to start it in silence; a case can change the input's fields, such as a burst's length, or give another signal, so short and long bursts are one spec; and `--response` writes the track filter's response as the Sound page draws it, so a type with no plugin to compare against is measured against its own drawing. A spec can have no `[reference]` and measure ours alone. **The Acid's filter** is inside the voice, which the probe can't feed a sweep without an engine hook into the Acid voice in `dsp/voices/acid.rs`, so it's read from a held sawtooth's harmonics on both sides, each times its number so a sawtooth's are level, once the filter's envelope has decayed with Env Mod and Decay at 0; the track's `Diode` type is the same ladder given a sweep. **The references:** Arturia's Filter M12 for the clean types, its cutoff in semitones so it's set to the note nearest our Cut, and Filter MINI for the Ladder, its knob set where its Res 90% peak falls on our Cut, found by halving; MINI doesn't self-oscillate, so M12's four-pole low-pass is the reference for ringing. Comp VCA-65 and Bus FORCE's compressor path, its other paths muted, for compressors. **Arturia's plugins open with the preset they last had**, and Acid V opened with "Carbon Based", whose sequencer plays a pattern from a held key, which is what #186's first cutoff numbers measured. A spec's `[reference]` can now name a factory preset, `preset = "Default"`, read from `/Library/Arturia/Presets`, and `state` for saved settings that aren't parameters, `Sequencer_OnOff = 0`, laid over the plugin's state by name as a host restores a project; Acid V takes a state only when given it twice. Acid V plays MIDI 36 an octave under ours, so its oscillator is raised 12 semitones. **Left for later:** fitting knob mappings, #295's among them, the 303's accent and slide set, saturation, and voices' envelopes. Added on 2026-11-10.
114. **The sample page plays what's on it through the cursor's track's chain: `;` the slice under the cursor, `Space c` from the cursor to the entry's end, and `i` a keys mode where the home row and the row above play slices `00` to `0F`, as tracker-ce's Beat Slice pads do; a key held plays only while it's down, and every voice playing the entry shown, the page's own and the track's notes, is a thin green line the terminal places at its frame's pixel as it plays** (#275, the seventh step of the sample editor's design, #268, M22). **Prior art:** tracker-ce's Sample Playback screen, read as prior art only, whose pads in Beat Slice mode each play a slice in order from the first pad (`padToBeatNoteConverter`), light the pads that have a slice, select the slice a pad plays (`modSliceSelect`), and let go of it when the pad is (`noteOffHandle`), and whose preview button plays while it's held; the MPC's sixteen pads in banks, pad 1 at the bottom left, and Elektron's sixteen trig keys; the program's own `;` keyboard on the track page, `a w s e d f` with `<` and `>` for the octave, and decision 53's hold; and Ableton's and Renoise's sample editors, which draw a playhead line over the waveform as a sample plays. **`Handle::play_file`** takes a span, or none for the whole file as the explorer plays it: from the span's start to its end, reversed if it says so, with its gain, pan, tune and fades, so the voice's reader plays it as it plays a slice in the song. The page sends the pool's own frames, an `Arc`, so nothing is loaded or copied, and the engine takes the file's length as the span's, so a reversed span turns round at the file's end. It's the explorer's preview voice, through the track's chain, so one sound plays at a time and the next fades out the last, as a track plays one note at a time. **`;`** plays the slice under the cursor as the song plays it, its span as `Playable` works it out on the edit thread with the slice's own gain, pan, tune, direction and fades, but not the track's `STA`, `SLEN` and `PLY`, so it's the slice's own frames; the selection waits for Visual mode (#276). **`Space c`** plays from the cursor's frame to the entry's end, or to the file's from past it, forward, at the entry's gain and tune, with a declick rather than the entry's fade in, as `Space c` plays the song from the cursor elsewhere. **Held,** after decision 53: down for `HOLD_TIME` or more, a key's sound stops when it's let go, through `StopFile`, and tapped, it plays to its end. The page counts what it's played, so a key let go stops only its own sound, not one a later key started. `Esc` on the page lets go of it, and `Tab` stays the song's transport. **The keys mode** is `i`, as the keys then play rather than command, as Insert's play notes in the phrase view, and `Esc` leaves it as it leaves Insert; `;` on the page plays the slice, so the track page's toggle can't be used. The home row from `a`, `a s d f g h j k`, plays slices `00` to `07`, and the row above from `q`, `q w e r t y u i`, plays `08` to `0F`: the home row first, as the issue asks and as an MPC's bottom row is its first pads, and sixteen, as an MPC's bank or Elektron's trig keys, a hex digit's worth of slices. `<` and `>` go a page of sixteen back and on, as they change octave on the track page's keyboard, no further than the last slice's page. A pad plays its slice as `;` does, held or tapped, and puts the cursor on the slice's start, as tracker-ce's pads select the slice they play; a pad past the last slice says so and plays nothing, and the status line names what the pads play. Every other key works on the page, so `;`, `l`, `0`, `zi` and the `Space` keys do, and leaving the page leaves the mode. **The playhead is a line,** after the author's comment on #274 that a cell's background is a character wide, about 15 pixels: a two-pixel line in the playhead's green, as it is everywhere, one image stored with `a=t` and placed with `a=p` once for each playhead, under placement ids 1 to 4, `X=` pixels into its character, after the ruler's marks and before the cursor's line, so the cursor is over one it meets. It's placed again only when it has moved a pixel, about 60 bytes, and taken off when its voice ends, so a page with nothing playing sends nothing. Like the cursor's line, it's sent again when the window changes size and taken off while anything is drawn over the page, and it follows `zc`: with the cursor as its column, it's its column's background in a dimmed green, as it is with no pictures drawn, in a render, and under anything drawn over the page. **What it shows:** each block's report says where up to four voices playing a sample through the watched track are (`Engine::playheads`), the track's note and its tail and then the preview voices, each with its bank entry, or none for a one-off file, in the pool's frames, and not a synth's or one fading out after it's cut. The page shows those of the entry shown, and the one-off file's only while it's what the page last played, so the explorer's file isn't drawn over another sample.
